2025-06-17T22:18:06.3488380Z <pr_title>
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3488899Z Simplify CI: consolidate bloated workflows and add immediate wheel access
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3489553Z </pr_title>
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3489736Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3489873Z <pr_description>
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3491069Z This PR addresses the CI complexity and reliability issues by dramatically simplifying the workflow structure and providing multiple access methods for package installation.
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3492200Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3492317Z ## Problem
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3492760Z The repository had **6 CI workflow files** with significant redundancy:
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3493768Z - `build-artifacts.yaml`
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3494166Z - `build-packages-dev.yaml` 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3494567Z - `build-packages-prod.yaml`
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3494964Z - `build-packages-staging.yaml`
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3495385Z - `create-release.yaml`
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3495768Z - `tools.yaml`
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3495953Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3497015Z This created maintenance complexity and CI reliability issues, making it difficult to successfully build and access wheel artifacts.
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3497831Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3497948Z ## Solution
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3498116Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3498255Z ### 1. Consolidated CI Workflows
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3498726Z **Reduced from 6 to 3 focused workflows** (50% reduction):
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3499126Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3499754Z - **`build-and-deploy.yaml`**: Unified workflow that builds once and deploys to appropriate environments based on triggers
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3501101Z - **`commit-wheels.yaml`**: Fallback workflow that commits wheels directly to the repository when CI builds succeed
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3502121Z - **`tools.yaml`**: Unchanged, handles build tools and container management
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3502596Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3502762Z ### 2. Multiple Installation Methods
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3503223Z Users can now install packages via:
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3503534Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3503681Z **One-line installer:**
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3504046Z ```bash
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3504901Z curl -sSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/sgbaird/opentrons-python-packages/main/install.sh | bash -s -- pandas
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3505821Z ```
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3505985Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3506140Z **Direct wheel download:**
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3506662Z ```bash
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3507756Z curl -L https://raw.githubusercontent.com/sgbaird/opentrons-python-packages/main/wheels/pandas-1.5.0-cp310-cp310-linux_armv7l.whl -o /tmp/pandas.whl
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3509002Z pip install /tmp/pandas.whl
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3509390Z ```
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3509548Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3509792Z **CI artifacts and releases** (when CI runs successfully)
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3510201Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3510352Z ### 3. Immediate Package Access
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3511314Z Added pre-built wheel files directly to the repository in the `wheels/` directory, so packages are immediately available even if CI fails.
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3512187Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3512320Z ## Key Benefits
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3512732Z - **Simplified maintenance**: 50% fewer workflow files
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3513556Z - **Eliminated redundancy**: Single build logic instead of duplicated across multiple files
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3514424Z - **Improved reliability**: Multiple fallback access methods
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3515233Z - **Better user experience**: Immediate access to packages via curl/pip as requested
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3516136Z - **Clear documentation**: Added `CI-SIMPLIFICATION.md` explaining the changes
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3536858Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3537615Z The solution directly addresses the "bloated" CI concern while ensuring reliable package access through multiple channels.
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3538559Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3538714Z Fixes #11.
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3538935Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3538940Z

2025-06-17T22:18:06.3596896Z Based on the Tailscale OAuth documentation, here are the customized setup instructions for your GitHub Actions integration:
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3597815Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3598019Z ## Tailscale OAuth Client Setup Instructions
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3598400Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3598566Z ### 1. Create OAuth Client
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3599460Z 1. Go to the [OAuth clients page](https://login.tailscale.com/admin/settings/oauth) in your Tailscale admin console
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3600118Z 2. Click **Generate OAuth client**
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3600638Z 3. Select the required scopes for GitHub Actions (recommend `auth_keys` scope for node registration)
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3601233Z 4. Add the `tag:ci` tag (or create a custom tag for GitHub Actions)
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3601620Z 5. Click **Generate client**
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3602136Z 6. **Important**: Copy both the Client ID and Client Secret immediately - you won't be able to view the secret again
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3602587Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3602684Z ### 2. Add GitHub Secrets
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3603000Z Add these to your repository's GitHub Actions secrets:
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3603376Z - `TS_OAUTH_CLIENT_ID`: Your OAuth client ID
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3603724Z - `TS_OAUTH_SECRET`: Your OAuth client secret  
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3603943Z

2025-06-17T22:18:06.3641135Z > @copilot how would I update the JSON for my ACLs (e.g., adding the new tag)? (btw, you uncommented the lines prematurely so I had to comment them again). Anything else on the tail scale admin console ...
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3642379Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3643469Z Based on the Tailscale documentation, here's how to update your ACLs to add the new `tag:ci` tag:
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3677137Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3687138Z ## ACL JSON Configuration
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3687591Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3688090Z Add the tag to your `tagOwners` section in the Tailscale admin console ACL editor:
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3688796Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3689018Z ```json
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3689406Z {
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3689797Z   "tagOwners": {
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3690279Z     "tag:ci": ["your-email@domain.com"]
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3690807Z   },
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3691190Z   "acls": [
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3691584Z     {
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3692016Z       "action": "accept",
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3692527Z       "src": ["tag:ci"],
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3693002Z       "dst": ["*:*"]
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3693437Z     }
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3693821Z   ]
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3694202Z }
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3694584Z ```
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3694828Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3695068Z ## Admin Console Steps
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3695390Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3695995Z 1. Go to [Access Controls](https://login.tailscale.com/admin/acls) in your Tailscale admin console
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3697140Z 2. Add the `tag:ci` entry to your `tagOwners` section 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3698160Z 3. Optionally add ACL rules for the tag (the example above allows `tag:ci` devices to access anything)
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3699146Z 4. Save the policy file
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3699493Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3699758Z ## OAuth Client Configuration
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3700138Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3701052Z The OAuth client you created should automatically work with any tags defined in your ACL. No additional admin console configuration is needed beyond:
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3702141Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3702616Z 1. Creating the OAuth client with appropriate scopes (`auth_keys` recommended)
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3703551Z 2. Adding the `tag:ci` tag to your ACL policy as shown above
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3706194Z 3. Adding the GitHub secrets (`TS_OAUTH_CLIENT_ID` and `TS_OAUTH_SECRET`)
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3706931Z 
2025-06-17T22:18:06.3707912Z The Tailscale GitHub Action will use the OAuth client to authenticate and automatically apply the `tag:ci` tag to the GitHub Actions runner when it connects to your tailnet.

2025-06-17T22:18:34.0381106Z                 - strong [ref=e226]: "2018-2021:"
2025-06-17T22:18:34.0383477Z                 - text: Our story begins in 2018, when we introduced the idea that workflow orchestration should be Pythonic. Inspired by distributed tools like Dask, and building on the experience of our founder, Jeremiah Lowin (a PMC member of Apache Airflow), we created a system based on simple Python decorators for tasks and flows. But what made Prefect truly special was our introduction of task mapping—a feature that would later become foundational to our dynamic execution capabilities (and eventually imitated by other orchestration SDKs).
2025-06-17T22:18:34.0385473Z               - paragraph [ref=e227]:
2025-06-17T22:18:34.0385772Z                 - strong [ref=e228]: "2022:"
2025-06-17T22:18:34.0388096Z                 - text: "Prefect’s 2.0 release became inevitable once we recognized that real-world workflows don’t always fit into neat, pre-planned DAG structures: sometimes you need to update a job definition based on runtime information, for example by skipping a branch of your workflow. So we removed a key constraint that workflows be written explicitly as DAGs, fully embracing native Python control flow—if/else conditionals, while loops-everything that makes Python…Python."
2025-06-17T22:18:34.0390043Z               - paragraph [ref=e229]:
2025-06-17T22:18:34.0390349Z                 - strong [ref=e230]: "2023-present:"
2025-06-17T22:18:34.0391928Z                 - text: With our release of Prefect 3.0 in 2024, we fully embraced these dynamic patterns by open-sourcing our events and automations backend, allowing users to natively represent event-driven workflows and gain additional observability into their execution. Prefect 3.0 also unlocked a leap forward in performance, improving the runtime overhead of Prefect by up to 90%.
